People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I'm here because of things at the warehouse. The routing system, specifically. It isn't just a computer to me anymore. I know it's been marking my moves, sending these messages through the hand scanners to push me out because I'm one of the old hands. 

I've been operating the trucks and tractors there for years. We used to drive these big propane ones, you know? Now they're all electric. Much quieter. Sometimes too quiet, honestly, you can't even hear them coming around the blind corners near the loading docks. My buddy Carl almost got clipped by one last week. Carl's got a bad knee, so he can't move out of the way like he used to. He tore his meniscus playing softball back in '98. We had a really great company team back then, won the regional league and everything. They gave us these cheap plastic trophies, but we were proud of them. I think my wife threw mine out when we moved to the new house. The basement there flooded twice in the first year, completely ruined the drywall... 

But anyway, the system. It knows I'm older, so the scanner keeps targeting me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.

Energetic? No, no, I haven't felt like that at all. Honestly, it's been the opposite most days. It's hard to find the energy to even drag myself out of bed, let alone feel high or wired. I used to have a lot of energy, though. Back when I was working the night shift, I could go on four hours of sleep, no problem. We'd finish up around 6 AM, and me and a few of the guys would head over to this diner on 4th Street. It's not there anymore, they tore it down to build a bank, which is a shame because they had the best hash browns. Crispy on the outside, soft on the inside. You can't find hash browns like that anymore. Now everywhere you go, it's just those frozen patties they deep fry. It's just not the same. My wife used to try and make them from scratch on Sunday mornings, but she'd always burn the onions. I never told her, though, because she'd get so upset. She's sensitive about her cooking ever since her mother criticized her pot roast at Thanksgiving back in '05... but no, I haven't had any of those energetic periods you're talking about. Just tired. Very tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, did you have any major illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Drugs? No, no drugs. I don't touch any of that stuff, never have. And no new medications either, just the same blood pressure pill I've been taking for years. The one with the little score mark down the middle. I have to break it in half because the full dose makes me dizzy when I stand up too fast. I learned that the hard way when I was fixing the gutter on the back porch last spring. Almost took a tumble right into the rose bushes. My wife planted those bushes when we first bought the house. They're these beautiful yellow roses, 'Peace' roses, I think she called them. They take a lot of upkeep, though. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom. The neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has these climbing roses that just take over everything. They're growing right up the side of her garage and into the power lines. I told her she ought to call the city to come trim them back before a storm knocks the power out for the whole block. We lost power for three days back during the ice storm of '98. Had to sleep in our winter coats and eat cold soup straight out of the can... but yeah, no drugs or new pills or anything like that. Just the regular one.

Energetic? No, no, I haven't felt like that at all. Honestly, it's been the opposite most days. It's hard to find the energy to even drag myself out of bed, let alone feel high or wired. I used to have a lot of energy, though. Back when I was working the night shift, I could go on four hours of sleep, no problem. We'd finish up around 6 AM, and me and a few of the guys would head over to this diner on 4th Street. It's not there anymore, they tore it down to build a bank, which is a shame because they had the best hash browns. Crispy on the outside, soft on the inside. You can't find hash browns like that anymore. Now everywhere you go, it's just those frozen patties they deep fry. It's just not the same. My wife used to try and make them from scratch on Sunday mornings, but she'd always burn the onions. I never told her, though, because she'd get so upset. She's sensitive about her cooking ever since her mother criticized her pot roast at Thanksgiving back in '05... but no, I haven't had any of those energetic periods you're talking about. Just tired. Very tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, did you have any major illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any drugs or new medications since this began?\n\nPatient: Drugs? No, no drugs. I don't touch any of that stuff, never have. And no new medications either, just the same blood pressure pill I've been taking for years. The one with the little score mark down the middle. I have to break it in half because the full dose makes me dizzy when I stand up too fast. I learned that the hard way when I was fixing the gutter on the back porch last spring. Almost took a tumble right into the rose bushes. My wife planted those bushes when we first bought the house. They're these beautiful yellow roses, 'Peace' roses, I think she called them. They take a lot of upkeep, though. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom. The neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has these climbing roses that just take over everything. They're growing right up the side of her garage and into the power lines. I told her she ought to call the city to come trim them back before a storm knocks the power out for the whole block. We lost power for three days back during the ice storm of '98. Had to sleep in our winter coats and eat cold soup straight out of the can... but yeah, no drugs or new pills or anything like that. Just the regular one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Hard to follow? Me? No, nobody's ever said that. I mean, my wife says I don't listen, but that's different. That's just marriage, right? You tune things out after thirty years. Like when she starts talking about her sister's dog. That dog is a menace, a little yappy terrier thing that bites at your ankles. They brought it over for the Fourth of July barbecue last year, and it chased a squirrel right through my wife's prized petunias. She was furious. Said the dog ruined the whole aesthetic of the patio. We spent a fortune on those patio pavers, too. Had a guy come out from over in Shelbyville to lay them. He did a good job, but he left his thermos behind. A big green Stanley thermos, the kind that keeps coffee hot all day. I tried calling the company to return it, but they went out of business a few months later. Shame, really, they did good work. Hard to find reliable contractors these days. Everyone's just looking to make a quick buck and cut corners. Like the guy who fixed my cousin's roof—used the wrong nails and the whole thing leaked the next time it rained... but no, nobody says my speech is hard to follow. I think I make perfect sense.

Yeah, yeah exactly. Like... I'll just be sitting out in my garage, right? Everything is completely shut off, dead quiet. But I hear the dispatch voices, clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous, saying security is on the way to come get me. It's just like the old two-way radios we used to have on the forklifts before they upgraded to the new digital system. Those old radios had terrible static. You'd be trying to hear if you needed to pull a pallet from aisle four, and all you'd get is this awful crackling noise. My buddy Phil, the one I told you about, he used to tape a piece of wire to the antenna thinking it would boost the signal. Didn't do a damn thing, of course. Phil was always coming up with these crazy fixes. He tried to fix a leaky radiator in his Chevy with an egg once. Read it in some magazine. The car smelled like sulfur for a month, and it still leaked. He ended up having to sell it for scrap. It was a shame, too, it was a classic, a '72 Chevelle. They don't make cars like that anymore. Heavy steel, you felt safe in them. Now everything is plastic and crumples if you look at it wrong. I saw a fender bender at the grocery store parking lot last week, and the whole front bumper just popped off like a Lego piece... but yeah, the voices. They just keep calling my number and saying security is coming. Even when I'm alone.

Ripped his shirt right off his back, lucky it wasn't his arm. They didn't have the same safety guards back then. We used to just eat our lunch sitting right on the pallets of dog food. The smell was terrible, especially in the summer when the AC broke... but yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started on me.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energetic, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: High? Energetic? No, no, I haven't felt like that at all. Honestly, it's been the opposite most days. It's hard to find the energy to even drag myself out of bed, let alone feel high or wired. I used to have a lot of energy, though. Back when I was working the night shift, I could go on four hours of sleep, no problem. We'd finish up around 6 AM, and me and a few of the guys would head over to this diner on 4th Street. It's not there anymore, they tore it down to build a bank, which is a shame because they had the best hash browns. Crispy on the outside, soft on the inside. You can't find hash browns like that anymore. Now everywhere you go, it's just those frozen patties they deep fry. It's just not the same. My wife used to try and make them from scratch on Sunday mornings, but she'd always burn the onions. I never told her, though, because she'd get so upset. She's sensitive about her cooking ever since her mother criticized her pot roast at Thanksgiving back in '05... but no, I haven't had any of those energetic periods you're talking about. Just tired. Very tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, did you have any major illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any drugs or new medications since this began?\n\nPatient: Drugs? No, no drugs. I don't touch any of that stuff, never have. And no new medications either, just the same blood pressure pill I've been taking for years. The one with the little score mark down the middle. I have to break it in half because the full dose makes me dizzy when I stand up too fast. I learned that the hard way when I was fixing the gutter on the back porch last spring. Almost took a tumble right into the rose bushes. My wife planted those bushes when we first bought the house. They're these beautiful yellow roses, 'Peace' roses, I think she called them. They take a lot of upkeep, though. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom. The neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has these climbing roses that just take over everything. They're growing right up the side of her garage and into the power lines. I told her she ought to call the city to come trim them back before a storm knocks the power out for the whole block. We lost power for three days back during the ice storm of '98. Had to sleep in our winter coats and eat cold soup straight out of the can... but yeah, no drugs or new pills or anything like that. Just the regular one.\n\nDoctor: Do other people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or jumps from topic to topic?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Me? No, nobody's ever said that. I mean, my wife says I don't listen, but that's different. That's just marriage, right? You tune things out after thirty years. Like when she starts talking about her sister's dog. That dog is a menace, a little yappy terrier thing that bites at your ankles. They brought it over for the Fourth of July barbecue last year, and it chased a squirrel right through my wife's prized petunias. She was furious. Said the dog ruined the whole aesthetic of the patio. We spent a fortune on those patio pavers, too. Had a guy come out from over in Shelbyville to lay them. He did a good job, but he left his thermos behind. A big green Stanley thermos, the kind that keeps coffee hot all day. I tried calling the company to return it, but they went out of business a few months later. Shame, really, they did good work. Hard to find reliable contractors these days. Everyone's just looking to make a quick buck and cut corners. Like the guy who fixed my cousin's roof—used the wrong nails and the whole thing leaked the next time it rained... but no, nobody says my speech is hard to follow. I think I make perfect sense.\n\nDoctor: When you say voices, do you hear people speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah exactly. Like... I'll just be sitting out in my garage, right? Everything is completely shut off, dead quiet. But I hear the dispatch voices, clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous, saying security is on the way to come get me. It's just like the old two-way radios we used to have on the forklifts before they upgraded to the new digital system. Those old radios had terrible static. You'd be trying to hear if you needed to pull a pallet from aisle four, and all you'd get is this awful crackling noise. My buddy Phil, the one I told you about, he used to tape a piece of wire to the antenna thinking it would boost the signal. Didn't do a damn thing, of course. Phil was always coming up with these crazy fixes. He tried to fix a leaky radiator in his Chevy with an egg once. Read it in some magazine. The car smelled like sulfur for a month, and it still leaked. He ended up having to sell it for scrap. It was a shame, too, it was a classic, a '72 Chevelle. They don't make cars like that anymore. Heavy steel, you felt safe in them. Now everything is plastic and crumples if you look at it wrong. I saw a fender bender at the grocery store parking lot last week, and the whole front bumper just popped off like a Lego piece... but yeah, the voices. They just keep calling my number and saying security is coming. Even when I'm alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I tried calling the company to return it, but they went out of business a few months later. Shame, really, they did good work. Hard to find reliable contractors these days. Everyone's just looking to make a quick buck and cut corners. Like the guy who fixed my cousin's roof—used the wrong nails and the whole thing leaked the next time it rained... but no, nobody says my speech is hard to follow. I think I make perfect sense.\n\nDoctor: When you say voices, do you hear people speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah exactly. Like... I'll just be sitting out in my garage, right? Everything is completely shut off, dead quiet. But I hear the dispatch voices, clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous, saying security is on the way to come get me. It's just like the old two-way radios we used to have on the forklifts before they upgraded to the new digital system. Those old radios had terrible static. You'd be trying to hear if you needed to pull a pallet from aisle four, and all you'd get is this awful crackling noise. My buddy Phil, the one I told you about, he used to tape a piece of wire to the antenna thinking it would boost the signal. Didn't do a damn thing, of course. Phil was always coming up with these crazy fixes. He tried to fix a leaky radiator in his Chevy with an egg once. Read it in some magazine. The car smelled like sulfur for a month, and it still leaked. He ended up having to sell it for scrap. It was a shame, too, it was a classic, a '72 Chevelle. They don't make cars like that anymore. Heavy steel, you felt safe in them. Now everything is plastic and crumples if you look at it wrong. I saw a fender bender at the grocery store parking lot last week, and the whole front bumper just popped off like a Lego piece... but yeah, the voices. They just keep calling my number and saying security is coming. Even when I'm alone.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 62 / industrial_truck_or_tractor_operator / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"Fishing, choir practice, and tinkering with wood in the garage used to settle me, but now I can look right at my rods or tools and feel no pull to touch them.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I... I guess I have. It's just... I used to love fishing. Used to go down to the lake every Sunday morning, early, before the sun was even fully up. Choir practice, too, on Wednesday nights. And I'd spend hours out in the garage tinkering with wood, making little birdhouses or fixing up old chairs. It used to really settle me, you know? Keep my hands busy. But now... I can walk right out there, look right at my fishing rods or my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them at all. It's like looking at someone else's stuff. I bought this beautiful piece of cherry wood a few months ago, thinking I'd make a new coffee table. Cherry is a real nice wood to work with, but you have to be careful when you stain it because it can get blotchy if you don't condition it first. My uncle Arthur, he was a master carpenter. Built his whole house from the ground up, just him and his two boys. They used oak for the floors, solid oak. That house is still standing today, solid as a rock. Arthur was a tough guy, fought in Korea. He brought back this old brass lighter from the war, used to let me play with it when I was a kid. You had to strike it just right or it wouldn't catch. They don't make things with that kind of quality anymore. Everything is disposable now. You buy a toaster, it breaks in a year, and you just throw it away instead of fixing it. It's a real shame, the amount of waste we produce. Landfills just filling up with cheap plastic junk... but anyway, no, I just don't care about the fishing or the woodworking anymore. I just sit there.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I'm here because of things at the warehouse. The routing system, specifically. It isn't just a computer to me anymore. I know it's been marking my moves, sending these messages through the hand scanners to push me out because I'm one of the old hands. \n\nI've been operating the trucks and tractors there for years. We used to drive these big propane ones, you know? Now they're all electric. Much quieter. Sometimes too quiet, honestly, you can't even hear them coming around the blind corners near the loading docks. My buddy Carl almost got clipped by one last week. Carl's got a bad knee, so he can't move out of the way like he used to. He tore his meniscus playing softball back in '98. We had a really great company team back then, won the regional league and everything. They gave us these cheap plastic trophies, but we were proud of them. I think my wife threw mine out when we moved to the new house. The basement there flooded twice in the first year, completely ruined the drywall... \n\nBut anyway, the system. It knows I'm older, so the scanner keeps targeting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed the routing system is targeting you?\n\nPatient: Well, the voices and the whole thing with the scanners... it's been about a year and eight months now. Started right around the time they brought in those new electric pallet jacks. I remember because the warehouse manager, Tom, made a big speech about efficiency and going green. Tom's a nice enough guy, I guess, but he wears these awful patterned ties. I mean, who wears a tie in a warehouse anyway? It's a safety hazard. If that thing gets caught in the conveyor belt by the packaging station, he's a goner. I saw a guy get his sleeve caught once back in the 80s. Ripped his shirt right off his back, lucky it wasn't his arm. They didn't have the same safety guards back then. We used to just eat our lunch sitting right on the pallets of dog food. The smell was terrible, especially in the summer when the AC broke... but yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started on me.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energetic, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: High? Energetic? No, no, I haven't felt like that at all. Honestly, it's been the opposite most days. It's hard to find the energy to even drag myself out of bed, let alone feel high or wired. I used to have a lot of energy, though. Back when I was working the night shift, I could go on four hours of sleep, no problem. We'd finish up around 6 AM, and me and a few of the guys would head over to this diner on 4th Street. It's not there anymore, they tore it down to build a bank, which is a shame because they had the best hash browns. Crispy on the outside, soft on the inside. You can't find hash browns like that anymore. Now everywhere you go, it's just those frozen patties they deep fry. It's just not the same. My wife used to try and make them from scratch on Sunday mornings, but she'd always burn the onions. I never told her, though, because she'd get so upset. She's sensitive about her cooking ever since her mother criticized her pot roast at Thanksgiving back in '05... but no, I haven't had any of those energetic periods you're talking about. Just tired. Very tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, did you have any major illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any drugs or new medications since this began?\n\nPatient: Drugs? No, no drugs. I don't touch any of that stuff, never have. manifestation: My wife says my face has gone flat and I hardly answer her, and most days I just sit at the kitchen table staring at my coffee instead of starting anything that needs doing.\n- key: Me? No, nobody's ever said that. I mean, my wife says I don't listen, but that's different. That's just marriage, right? You tune things out after thirty years. Like when she starts talking about her sister's dog. That dog is a menace, a little yappy terrier thing that bites at your ankles. They brought it over for the Fourth of July barbecue last year, and it chased a squirrel right through my wife's prized petunias. She was furious. Said the dog ruined the whole aesthetic of the patio. We spent a fortune on those patio pavers, too. Had a guy come out from over in Shelbyville to lay them. He did a good job, but he left his thermos behind. A big green Stanley thermos, the kind that keeps coffee hot all day. I tried calling the company to return it, but they went out of business a few months later. Shame, really, they did good work. Hard to find reliable contractors these days. Everyone's just looking to make a quick buck and cut corners. Everything is completely shut off, dead quiet. But I hear the dispatch voices, clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous, saying security is on the way to come get me. It's just like the old two-way radios we used to have on the forklifts before they upgraded to the new digital system. Those old radios had terrible static. You'd be trying to hear if you needed to pull a pallet from aisle four, and all you'd get is this awful crackling noise. My buddy Phil, the one I told you about, he used to tape a piece of wire to the antenna thinking it would boost the signal. Didn't do a damn thing, of course. Phil was always coming up with these crazy fixes. He tried to fix a leaky radiator in his Chevy with an egg once. Read it in some magazine. The car smelled like sulfur for a month, and it still leaked. He ended up having to sell it for scrap. It was a shame, too, it was a classic, a '72 Chevelle. They don't make cars like that anymore. Heavy steel, you felt safe in them. Now everything is plastic and crumples if you look at it wrong. I saw a fender bender at the grocery store parking lot last week, and the whole front bumper just popped off like a Lego piece... but yeah, the voices. They just keep calling my number and saying security is coming. Even when I'm alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess I have. It's just... I used to love fishing. Used to go down to the lake every Sunday morning, early, before the sun was even fully up. Choir practice, too, on Wednesday nights. And I'd spend hours out in the garage tinkering with wood, making little birdhouses or fixing up old chairs. It used to really settle me, you know? Keep my hands busy. But now... I can walk right out there, look right at my fishing rods or my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them at all. It's like looking at someone else's stuff. I bought this beautiful piece of cherry wood a few months ago, thinking I'd make a new coffee table. Cherry is a real nice wood to work with, but you have to be careful when you stain it because it can get blotchy if you don't condition it first. My uncle Arthur, he was a master carpenter. Built his whole house from the ground up, just him and his two boys. They used oak for the floors, solid oak. That house is still standing today, solid as a rock. Arthur was a tough guy, fought in Korea. He brought back this old brass lighter from the war, used to let me play with it when I was a kid. You had to strike it just right or it wouldn't catch. They don't make things with that kind of quality anymore. Everything is disposable now. You buy a toaster, it breaks in a year, and you just throw it away instead of fixing it. It's a real shame, the amount of waste we produce. Landfills just filling up with cheap plastic junk... but anyway, no, I just don't care about the fishing or the woodworking anymore. I just sit there.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen the voices talk to you, do they ever say things like that you deserve to be punished or that you should die?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, no, they don't say that. Nothing about dying or... or being punished. Just that I'm dangerous and security is coming to get me. Getting in trouble at the warehouse was a big deal, though, if you actually messed up. We had this safety inspector, an OSHA guy, who used to come around twice a year. The portions are smaller, and they charge you an arm and a leg just for a combo meal. I went to a burger joint down the street last month and it was almost fifteen bucks just for myself. Used to be you could get a whole meal for a five-dollar bill and still have change for the jukebox... but, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, the voices. No, they never say I should die or anything like that. Just the dispatch calling my number.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I'm here because of things at the warehouse. The routing system, specifically. It isn't just a computer to me anymore. I know it's been marking my moves, sending these messages through the hand scanners to push me out because I'm one of the old hands. \n\nI've been operating the trucks and tractors there for years. We used to drive these big propane ones, you know? Now they're all electric. Much quieter. Sometimes too quiet, honestly, you can't even hear them coming around the blind corners near the loading docks. My buddy Carl almost got clipped by one last week. Carl's got a bad knee, so he can't move out of the way like he used to. He tore his meniscus playing softball back in '98. We had a really great company team back then, won the regional league and everything. They gave us these cheap plastic trophies, but we were proud of them. I think my wife threw mine out when we moved to the new house. The basement there flooded twice in the first year, completely ruined the drywall... \n\nBut anyway, the system. It knows I'm older, so the scanner keeps targeting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed the routing system is targeting you?\n\nPatient: Well, the voices and the whole thing with the scanners... it's been about a year and eight months now. Started right around the time they brought in those new electric pallet jacks. I remember because the warehouse manager, Tom, made a big speech about efficiency and going green. Tom's a nice enough guy, I guess, but he wears these awful patterned ties. I mean, who wears a tie in a warehouse anyway? It's a safety hazard. If that thing gets caught in the conveyor belt by the packaging station, he's a goner. I saw a guy get his sleeve caught once back in the 80s. Ripped his shirt right off his back, lucky it wasn't his arm. They didn't have the same safety guards back then. We used to just eat our lunch sitting right on the pallets of dog food. The smell was terrible, especially in the summer when the AC broke... but yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started on me.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energetic, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: High? Energetic? No, no, I haven't felt like that at all. Honestly, it's been the opposite most days. It's hard to find the energy to even drag myself out of bed, let alone feel high or wired. I used to have a lot of energy, though. Back when I was working the night shift, I could go on four hours of sleep, no problem. We'd finish up around 6 AM, and me and a few of the guys would head over to this diner on 4th Street. It's not there anymore, they tore it down to build a bank, which is a shame because they had the best hash browns. Crispy on the outside, soft on the inside. You can't find hash browns like that anymore. Now everywhere you go, it's just those frozen patties they deep fry. It's just not the same. My wife used to try and make them from scratch on Sunday mornings, but she'd always burn the onions. I never told her, though, because she'd get so upset. She's sensitive about her cooking ever since her mother criticized her pot roast at Thanksgiving back in '05... but no, I haven't had any of those energetic periods you're talking about. Just tired. Very tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, did you have any major illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any drugs or new medications since this began?\n\nPatient: Drugs? No, no drugs. I don't touch any of that stuff, never have. And no new medications either, just the same blood pressure pill I've been taking for years. The one with the little score mark down the middle. I have to break it in half because the full dose makes me dizzy when I stand up too fast. I learned that the hard way when I was fixing the gutter on the back porch last spring. Almost took a tumble right into the rose bushes. My wife planted those bushes when we first bought the house. They're these beautiful yellow roses, 'Peace' roses, I think she called them. They take a lot of upkeep, though. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom. The neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has these climbing roses that just take over everything. They're growing right up the side of her garage and into the power lines. I told her she ought to call the city to come trim them back before a storm knocks the power out for the whole block. We lost power for three days back during the ice storm of '98. Had to sleep in our winter coats and eat cold soup straight out of the can... but yeah, no drugs or new pills or anything like that. Just the regular one.\n\nDoctor: Do other people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or jumps from topic to topic?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Me? No, nobody's ever said that. I mean, my wife says I don't listen, but that's different. That's just marriage, right? You tune things out after thirty years. Like when she starts talking about her sister's dog. That dog is a menace, a little yappy terrier thing that bites at your ankles. They brought it over for the Fourth of July barbecue last year, and it chased a squirrel right through my wife's prized petunias. She was furious. Said the dog ruined the whole aesthetic of the patio. We spent a fortune on those patio pavers, too. Had a guy come out from over in Shelbyville to lay them. He did a good job, but he left his thermos behind. A big green Stanley thermos, the kind that keeps coffee hot all day. I tried calling the company to return it, but they went out of business a few months later. Shame, really, they did good work. Hard to find reliable contractors these days. Everyone's just looking to make a quick buck and cut corners. Like the guy who fixed my cousin's roof—used the wrong nails and the whole thing leaked the next time it rained... but no, nobody says my speech is hard to follow. I think I make perfect sense.\n\nDoctor: When you say voices, do you hear people speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah exactly. Like... I'll just be sitting out in my garage, right? Everything is completely shut off, dead quiet. But I hear the dispatch voices, clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous, saying security is on the way to come get me. It's just like the old two-way radios we used to have on the forklifts before they upgraded to the new digital system. Those old radios had terrible static. You'd be trying to hear if you needed to pull a pallet from aisle four, and all you'd get is this awful crackling noise. My buddy Phil, the one I told you about, he used to tape a piece of wire to the antenna thinking it would boost the signal. Didn't do a damn thing, of course. Phil was always coming up with these crazy fixes. He tried to fix a leaky radiator in his Chevy with an egg once. Read it in some magazine. The car smelled like sulfur for a month, and it still leaked. He ended up having to sell it for scrap. It was a shame, too, it was a classic, a '72 Chevelle. They don't make cars like that anymore. Heavy steel, you felt safe in them. Now everything is plastic and crumples if you look at it wrong. I saw a fender bender at the grocery store parking lot last week, and the whole front bumper just popped off like a Lego piece... but yeah, the voices. They just keep calling my number and saying security is coming. Even when I'm alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess I have. It's just... I used to love fishing. Used to go down to the lake every Sunday morning, early, before the sun was even fully up. Choir practice, too, on Wednesday nights. And I'd spend hours out in the garage tinkering with wood, making little birdhouses or fixing up old chairs. It used to really settle me, you know? Keep my hands busy. But now... I can walk right out there, look right at my fishing rods or my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them at all. It's like looking at someone else's stuff. I bought this beautiful piece of cherry wood a few months ago, thinking I'd make a new coffee table. Cherry is a real nice wood to work with, but you have to be careful when you stain it because it can get blotchy if you don't condition it first. My uncle Arthur, he was a master carpenter. Built his whole house from the ground up, just him and his two boys. They used oak for the floors, solid oak. That house is still standing today, solid as a rock. Arthur was a tough guy, fought in Korea. He brought back this old brass lighter from the war, used to let me play with it when I was a kid. You had to strike it just right or it wouldn't catch. They don't make things with that kind of quality anymore. Everything is disposable now. You buy a toaster, it breaks in a year, and you just throw it away instead of fixing it. It's a real shame, the amount of waste we produce. Landfills just filling up with cheap plastic junk... but anyway, no, I just don't care about the fishing or the woodworking anymore. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: When the voices talk to you, do they ever say things like that you deserve to be punished or that you should die?\n\nPatient: No, no, they don't say that. Nothing about dying or... or being punished. Just that I'm dangerous and security is coming to get me. Getting in trouble at the warehouse was a big deal, though, if you actually messed up. We had this safety inspector, an OSHA guy, who used to come around twice a year. You could always tell he didn't actually work on the floor because his hard hat was spotless. Not a single scuff on it. He always carried this heavy aluminum clipboard, clicking his pen. I actually had a clipboard just like that for a while. Used it to keep track of the scores for my bowling league. We bowled every Thursday night over at Pinewood Lanes. Man, that place had a very specific smell... a mix of stale beer, floor wax, and that spray they put in the rental shoes. But they had the absolute best french fries. Crinkle cut. You really don't see crinkle cut fries much anymore, do you? Everywhere you go now it's those seasoned curly fries, or the shoestring ones that get cold in two minutes. Fast food just isn't the same. The portions are smaller, and they charge you an arm and a leg just for a combo meal. I went to a burger joint down the street last month and it was almost fifteen bucks just for myself. Used to be you could get a whole meal for a five-dollar bill and still have change for the jukebox... but, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, the voices. No, they never say I should die or anything like that. Just the dispatch calling my number.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, they never say I should die or anything like that. At home, my wife has had to take over bills, appointments, and most chores because I cannot keep up with them reliably.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, it did. After that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties completely. Put me on leave. They said I just wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is crazy, because I've driven those things for thirty years. Thirty years! You get to know a machine after that long. You know the exact sound the hydraulics make when they need fluid. It's this high-pitched whine, almost like a mosquito right in your ear. My neighbor's kid, little Tommy, he got this electric scooter for Christmas, and it makes that exact same whining noise. Zips up and down the sidewalk all afternoon. I don't know how his parents stand it. Kids these days, they don't play outside like we used to, though, right? Unless it's got a motor or a screen. We used to play kick the can until the streetlights came on. You had to be fast, too, because old Mrs. Higgins down the block would call the cops if you stepped on her prize-winning petunias. She was serious about those flowers. Won a ribbon at the county fair three years running. The fair used to be such a big deal. They had this funnel cake stand that made them fresh, piping hot, covered in powdered sugar. You'd get sugar all down your shirt, but it was worth it. Now the fair is just overpriced rides that look like they're going to fall apart... but yeah, I'm on leave now. And even at home, I just can't keep up. My wife has had to take over paying all the bills, keeping track of my appointments, even most of the chores around the house. I just... I can't seem to manage it reliably anymore.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F22, F06.2, F43.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I'm here because of things at the warehouse. The routing system, specifically. It isn't just a computer to me anymore. I know it's been marking my moves, sending these messages through the hand scanners to push me out because I'm one of the old hands. \n\nI've been operating the trucks and tractors there for years. We used to drive these big propane ones, you know? Now they're all electric. Much quieter. Sometimes too quiet, honestly, you can't even hear them coming around the blind corners near the loading docks. My buddy Carl almost got clipped by one last week. Carl's got a bad knee, so he can't move out of the way like he used to. He tore his meniscus playing softball back in '98. We had a really great company team back then, won the regional league and everything. They gave us these cheap plastic trophies, but we were proud of them. I think my wife threw mine out when we moved to the new house. The basement there flooded twice in the first year, completely ruined the drywall... \n\nBut anyway, the system. It knows I'm older, so the scanner keeps targeting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed the routing system is targeting you?\n\nPatient: Well, the voices and the whole thing with the scanners... it's been about a year and eight months now. Started right around the time they brought in those new electric pallet jacks. I remember because the warehouse manager, Tom, made a big speech about efficiency and going green. Tom's a nice enough guy, I guess, but he wears these awful patterned ties. I mean, who wears a tie in a warehouse anyway? It's a safety hazard. If that thing gets caught in the conveyor belt by the packaging station, he's a goner. I saw a guy get his sleeve caught once back in the 80s. Ripped his shirt right off his back, lucky it wasn't his arm. They didn't have the same safety guards back then. We used to just eat our lunch sitting right on the pallets of dog food. The smell was terrible, especially in the summer when the AC broke... but yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started on me.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energetic, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: High? Energetic? No, no, I haven't felt like that at all. Honestly, it's been the opposite most days. It's hard to find the energy to even drag myself out of bed, let alone feel high or wired. I used to have a lot of energy, though. Back when I was working the night shift, I could go on four hours of sleep, no problem. We'd finish up around 6 AM, and me and a few of the guys would head over to this diner on 4th Street. It's not there anymore, they tore it down to build a bank, which is a shame because they had the best hash browns. Crispy on the outside, soft on the inside. You can't find hash browns like that anymore. Now everywhere you go, it's just those frozen patties they deep fry. It's just not the same. My wife used to try and make them from scratch on Sunday mornings, but she'd always burn the onions. I never told her, though, because she'd get so upset. She's sensitive about her cooking ever since her mother criticized her pot roast at Thanksgiving back in '05... but no, I haven't had any of those energetic periods you're talking about. Just tired. Very tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, did you have any major illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any drugs or new medications since this began?\n\nPatient: Drugs? No, no drugs. I don't touch any of that stuff, never have. And no new medications either, just the same blood pressure pill I've been taking for years. The one with the little score mark down the middle. I have to break it in half because the full dose makes me dizzy when I stand up too fast. I learned that the hard way when I was fixing the gutter on the back porch last spring. Almost took a tumble right into the rose bushes. My wife planted those bushes when we first bought the house. They're these beautiful yellow roses, 'Peace' roses, I think she called them. They take a lot of upkeep, though. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom. The neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has these climbing roses that just take over everything. They're growing right up the side of her garage and into the power lines. I told her she ought to call the city to come trim them back before a storm knocks the power out for the whole block. We lost power for three days back during the ice storm of '98. Had to sleep in our winter coats and eat cold soup straight out of the can... but yeah, no drugs or new pills or anything like that. Just the regular one.\n\nDoctor: Do other people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or jumps from topic to topic?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Me? No, nobody's ever said that. I mean, my wife says I don't listen, but that's different. That's just marriage, right? You tune things out after thirty years. Like when she starts talking about her sister's dog. That dog is a menace, a little yappy terrier thing that bites at your ankles. They brought it over for the Fourth of July barbecue last year, and it chased a squirrel right through my wife's prized petunias. She was furious. Said the dog ruined the whole aesthetic of the patio. We spent a fortune on those patio pavers, too. Had a guy come out from over in Shelbyville to lay them. He did a good job, but he left his thermos behind. A big green Stanley thermos, the kind that keeps coffee hot all day. I tried calling the company to return it, but they went out of business a few months later. Shame, really, they did good work. Hard to find reliable contractors these days. Everyone's just looking to make a quick buck and cut corners. Like the guy who fixed my cousin's roof—used the wrong nails and the whole thing leaked the next time it rained... but no, nobody says my speech is hard to follow. I think I make perfect sense.\n\nDoctor: When you say voices, do you hear people speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah exactly. Like... I'll just be sitting out in my garage, right? Everything is completely shut off, dead quiet. But I hear the dispatch voices, clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous, saying security is on the way to come get me. It's just like the old two-way radios we used to have on the forklifts before they upgraded to the new digital system. Those old radios had terrible static. You'd be trying to hear if you needed to pull a pallet from aisle four, and all you'd get is this awful crackling noise. My buddy Phil, the one I told you about, he used to tape a piece of wire to the antenna thinking it would boost the signal. Didn't do a damn thing, of course. Phil was always coming up with these crazy fixes. He tried to fix a leaky radiator in his Chevy with an egg once. Read it in some magazine. The car smelled like sulfur for a month, and it still leaked. He ended up having to sell it for scrap. It was a shame, too, it was a classic, a '72 Chevelle. They don't make cars like that anymore. Heavy steel, you felt safe in them. Now everything is plastic and crumples if you look at it wrong. I saw a fender bender at the grocery store parking lot last week, and the whole front bumper just popped off like a Lego piece... but yeah, the voices. They just keep calling my number and saying security is coming. Even when I'm alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess I have. It's just... I used to love fishing. Used to go down to the lake every Sunday morning, early, before the sun was even fully up. Choir practice, too, on Wednesday nights. And I'd spend hours out in the garage tinkering with wood, making little birdhouses or fixing up old chairs. It used to really settle me, you know? Keep my hands busy. But now... I can walk right out there, look right at my fishing rods or my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them at all. It's like looking at someone else's stuff. I bought this beautiful piece of cherry wood a few months ago, thinking I'd make a new coffee table. Cherry is a real nice wood to work with, but you have to be careful when you stain it because it can get blotchy if you don't condition it first. My uncle Arthur, he was a master carpenter. Built his whole house from the ground up, just him and his two boys. They used oak for the floors, solid oak. That house is still standing today, solid as a rock. Arthur was a tough guy, fought in Korea. He brought back this old brass lighter from the war, used to let me play with it when I was a kid. You had to strike it just right or it wouldn't catch. They don't make things with that kind of quality anymore. Everything is disposable now. You buy a toaster, it breaks in a year, and you just throw it away instead of fixing it. It's a real shame, the amount of waste we produce. Landfills just filling up with cheap plastic junk... but anyway, no, I just don't care about the fishing or the woodworking anymore. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: When the voices talk to you, do they ever say things like that you deserve to be punished or that you should die?\n\nPatient: No, no, they don't say that. Nothing about dying or... or being punished. Just that I'm dangerous and security is coming to get me. Getting in trouble at the warehouse was a big deal, though, if you actually messed up. We had this safety inspector, an OSHA guy, who used to come around twice a year. You could always tell he didn't actually work on the floor because his hard hat was spotless. Not a single scuff on it. He always carried this heavy aluminum clipboard, clicking his pen. I actually had a clipboard just like that for a while. Used it to keep track of the scores for my bowling league. We bowled every Thursday night over at Pinewood Lanes. Man, that place had a very specific smell... a mix of stale beer, floor wax, and that spray they put in the rental shoes. But they had the absolute best french fries. Crinkle cut. You really don't see crinkle cut fries much anymore, do you? Everywhere you go now it's those seasoned curly fries, or the shoestring ones that get cold in two minutes. Fast food just isn't the same. The portions are smaller, and they charge you an arm and a leg just for a combo meal. I went to a burger joint down the street last month and it was almost fifteen bucks just for myself. Used to be you could get a whole meal for a five-dollar bill and still have change for the jukebox... but, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, the voices. No, they never say I should die or anything like that. Just the dispatch calling my number.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the problem with the scanners and voices, has your ability to do your job gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it did. After that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties completely. Put me on leave. They said I just wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is crazy, because I've driven those things for thirty years. Thirty years! You get to know a machine after that long. You know the exact sound the hydraulics make when they need fluid. It's this high-pitched whine, almost like a mosquito right in your ear. My neighbor's kid, little Tommy, he got this electric scooter for Christmas, and it makes that exact same whining noise. Zips up and down the sidewalk all afternoon. I don't know how his parents stand it. Kids these days, they don't play outside like we used to, though, right? Unless it's got a motor or a screen. We used to play kick the can until the streetlights came on. You had to be fast, too, because old Mrs. Higgins down the block would call the cops if you stepped on her prize-winning petunias. She was serious about those flowers. Won a ribbon at the county fair three years running. The fair used to be such a big deal. They had this funnel cake stand that made them fresh, piping hot, covered in powdered sugar. You'd get sugar all down your shirt, but it was worth it. Now the fair is just overpriced rides that look like they're going to fall apart... but yeah, I'm on leave now. And even at home, I just can't keep up. My wife has had to take over paying all the bills, keeping track of my appointments, even most of the chores around the house. I just... I can't seem to manage it reliably anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They had this funnel cake stand that made them fresh, piping hot, covered in powdered sugar. You'd get sugar all down your shirt, but it was worth it. Now the fair is just overpriced rides that look like they're going to fall apart... but yeah, I'm on leave now. And even at home, I just can't keep up. My wife has had to take over paying all the bills, keeping track of my appointments, even most of the chores around the house. I just... I can't seem to manage it reliably anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, my wife... she actually brought that up just the other day. She says my face has gone flat, like a board. Says I hardly answer her when she talks to me. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, just staring at my coffee instead of starting anything that needs doing. It's funny, I used to drink my coffee black, just straight black. But then a few years ago, my stomach started acting up, giving me this awful heartburn. The doctor told me to cut back on the acid, so I started putting a little splash of whole milk in it. Not cream, cream is too heavy. Just milk. We used to get milk delivered to the house when I was a boy. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, he drove this white truck with a picture of a cow on the side. He'd leave the glass bottles right on the porch in a little metal wire carrier. You had to bring the empty bottles back out the next morning so he could take them back to the dairy to be washed and reused. It made a lot of sense, really. Good for the environment, not like all these plastic jugs we use now that just end up floating in the ocean somewhere. I saw a documentary on TV about the ocean, about how there's this giant patch of garbage just swirling around out there in the Pacific. It's the size of Texas, they said. Can you imagine that? Just miles and miles of plastic bags and soda rings and old fishing nets. It makes you wonder what kind of world we're leaving for the next generation. My grandson, he's only four, and he loves those little juice boxes, you know the ones with the tiny straws wrapped in plastic? We buy them by the case. It's convenient, sure, but... what was I saying? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, she says I just look blank now. And I just sit there with my coffee.
