    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F25.1\", \"F20.9\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I'm here because of things at the warehouse. The routing system, specifically. It isn't just a computer to me anymore. I know it's been marking my moves, sending these messages through the hand scanners to push me out because I'm one of the old hands. \n\nI've been operating the trucks and tractors there for years. We used to drive these big propane ones, you know? Now they're all electric. Much quieter. Sometimes too quiet, honestly, you can't even hear them coming around the blind corners near the loading docks. My buddy Carl almost got clipped by one last week. Carl's got a bad knee, so he can't move out of the way like he used to. He tore his meniscus playing softball back in '98. We had a really great company team back then, won the regional league and everything. They gave us these cheap plastic trophies, but we were proud of them. I think my wife threw mine out when we moved to the new house. The basement there flooded twice in the first year, completely ruined the drywall... \n\nBut anyway, the system. It knows I'm older, so the scanner keeps targeting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed the routing system is targeting you?\n\nPatient: Well, the voices and the whole thing with the scanners... it's been about a year and eight months now. Started right around the time they brought in those new electric pallet jacks. I remember because the warehouse manager, Tom, made a big speech about efficiency and going green. Tom's a nice enough guy, I guess, but he wears these awful patterned ties. I mean, who wears a tie in a warehouse anyway? It's a safety hazard. If that thing gets caught in the conveyor belt by the packaging station, he's a goner. I saw a guy get his sleeve caught once back in the 80s. Ripped his shirt right off his back, lucky it wasn't his arm. They didn't have the same safety guards back then. We used to just eat our lunch sitting right on the pallets of dog food. The smell was terrible, especially in the summer when the AC broke... but yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started on me.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energetic, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: High? Energetic? No, no, I haven't felt like that at all. Honestly, it's been the opposite most days. It's hard to find the energy to even drag myself out of bed, let alone feel high or wired. I used to have a lot of energy, though. Back when I was working the night shift, I could go on four hours of sleep, no problem. We'd finish up around 6 AM, and me and a few of the guys would head over to this diner on 4th Street. It's not there anymore, they tore it down to build a bank, which is a shame because they had the best hash browns. Crispy on the outside, soft on the inside. You can't find hash browns like that anymore. Now everywhere you go, it's just those frozen patties they deep fry. It's just not the same. My wife used to try and make them from scratch on Sunday mornings, but she'd always burn the onions. I never told her, though, because she'd get so upset. She's sensitive about her cooking ever since her mother criticized her pot roast at Thanksgiving back in '05... but no, I haven't had any of those energetic periods you're talking about. Just tired. Very tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, did you have any major illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major illnesses. And no head injuries, thank god. I've always been pretty careful about my head. You have to be, working around heavy machinery. Hard hats are mandatory now, of course, but it wasn't always like that. I remember when they first introduced the hard hat rule, oh, must have been '86 or '87. Half the guys complained it messed up their hair or gave them a headache. My buddy Phil refused to wear his for a week until the foreman threatened to dock his pay. Phil was stubborn like that. He drove a forklift like a maniac, always taking the corners too fast. He actually knocked over a whole pallet of canned peaches once. Took us three hours to clean up the syrup. The smell of peaches makes me sick to my stomach to this day. My wife bought some peach preserves last month and I had to ask her to throw them out. She bought them at that farmer's market downtown, the one they set up by the old courthouse on Saturdays. Parking down there is a nightmare, though. The city really needs to build another garage... but no, my head is fine. No injuries.\n\nDoctor: Have you started using any drugs or new medications since this began?\n\nPatient: Drugs? No, no drugs. I don't touch any of that stuff, never have. And no new medications either, just the same blood pressure pill I've been taking for years. The one with the little score mark down the middle. I have to break it in half because the full dose makes me dizzy when I stand up too fast. I learned that the hard way when I was fixing the gutter on the back porch last spring. Almost took a tumble right into the rose bushes. My wife planted those bushes when we first bought the house. They're these beautiful yellow roses, 'Peace' roses, I think she called them. They take a lot of upkeep, though. You have to prune them just right or they get all leggy and don't bloom. The neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she has these climbing roses that just take over everything. They're growing right up the side of her garage and into the power lines. I told her she ought to call the city to come trim them back before a storm knocks the power out for the whole block. We lost power for three days back during the ice storm of '98. Had to sleep in our winter coats and eat cold soup straight out of the can... but yeah, no drugs or new pills or anything like that. Just the regular one.\n\nDoctor: Do other people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or jumps from topic to topic?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Me? No, nobody's ever said that. I mean, my wife says I don't listen, but that's different. That's just marriage, right? You tune things out after thirty years. Like when she starts talking about her sister's dog. That dog is a menace, a little yappy terrier thing that bites at your ankles. They brought it over for the Fourth of July barbecue last year, and it chased a squirrel right through my wife's prized petunias. She was furious. Said the dog ruined the whole aesthetic of the patio. We spent a fortune on those patio pavers, too. Had a guy come out from over in Shelbyville to lay them. He did a good job, but he left his thermos behind. A big green Stanley thermos, the kind that keeps coffee hot all day. I tried calling the company to return it, but they went out of business a few months later. Shame, really, they did good work. Hard to find reliable contractors these days. Everyone's just looking to make a quick buck and cut corners. Like the guy who fixed my cousin's roof—used the wrong nails and the whole thing leaked the next time it rained... but no, nobody says my speech is hard to follow. I think I make perfect sense.\n\nDoctor: When you say voices, do you hear people speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah exactly. Like... I'll just be sitting out in my garage, right? Everything is completely shut off, dead quiet. But I hear the dispatch voices, clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous, saying security is on the way to come get me. It's just like the old two-way radios we used to have on the forklifts before they upgraded to the new digital system. Those old radios had terrible static. You'd be trying to hear if you needed to pull a pallet from aisle four, and all you'd get is this awful crackling noise. My buddy Phil, the one I told you about, he used to tape a piece of wire to the antenna thinking it would boost the signal. Didn't do a damn thing, of course. Phil was always coming up with these crazy fixes. He tried to fix a leaky radiator in his Chevy with an egg once. Read it in some magazine. The car smelled like sulfur for a month, and it still leaked. He ended up having to sell it for scrap. It was a shame, too, it was a classic, a '72 Chevelle. They don't make cars like that anymore. Heavy steel, you felt safe in them. Now everything is plastic and crumples if you look at it wrong. I saw a fender bender at the grocery store parking lot last week, and the whole front bumper just popped off like a Lego piece... but yeah, the voices. They just keep calling my number and saying security is coming. Even when I'm alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I guess I have. It's just... I used to love fishing. Used to go down to the lake every Sunday morning, early, before the sun was even fully up. Choir practice, too, on Wednesday nights. And I'd spend hours out in the garage tinkering with wood, making little birdhouses or fixing up old chairs. It used to really settle me, you know? Keep my hands busy. But now... I can walk right out there, look right at my fishing rods or my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them at all. It's like looking at someone else's stuff. I bought this beautiful piece of cherry wood a few months ago, thinking I'd make a new coffee table. Cherry is a real nice wood to work with, but you have to be careful when you stain it because it can get blotchy if you don't condition it first. My uncle Arthur, he was a master carpenter. Built his whole house from the ground up, just him and his two boys. They used oak for the floors, solid oak. That house is still standing today, solid as a rock. Arthur was a tough guy, fought in Korea. He brought back this old brass lighter from the war, used to let me play with it when I was a kid. You had to strike it just right or it wouldn't catch. They don't make things with that kind of quality anymore. Everything is disposable now. You buy a toaster, it breaks in a year, and you just throw it away instead of fixing it. It's a real shame, the amount of waste we produce. Landfills just filling up with cheap plastic junk... but anyway, no, I just don't care about the fishing or the woodworking anymore. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: When the voices talk to you, do they ever say things like that you deserve to be punished or that you should die?\n\nPatient: No, no, they don't say that. Nothing about dying or... or being punished. Just that I'm dangerous and security is coming to get me. Getting in trouble at the warehouse was a big deal, though, if you actually messed up. We had this safety inspector, an OSHA guy, who used to come around twice a year. You could always tell he didn't actually work on the floor because his hard hat was spotless. Not a single scuff on it. He always carried this heavy aluminum clipboard, clicking his pen. I actually had a clipboard just like that for a while. Used it to keep track of the scores for my bowling league. We bowled every Thursday night over at Pinewood Lanes. Man, that place had a very specific smell... a mix of stale beer, floor wax, and that spray they put in the rental shoes. But they had the absolute best french fries. Crinkle cut. You really don't see crinkle cut fries much anymore, do you? Everywhere you go now it's those seasoned curly fries, or the shoestring ones that get cold in two minutes. Fast food just isn't the same. The portions are smaller, and they charge you an arm and a leg just for a combo meal. I went to a burger joint down the street last month and it was almost fifteen bucks just for myself. Used to be you could get a whole meal for a five-dollar bill and still have change for the jukebox... but, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, the voices. No, they never say I should die or anything like that. Just the dispatch calling my number.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the problem with the scanners and voices, has your ability to do your job gotten worse?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it did. After that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties completely. Put me on leave. They said I just wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is crazy, because I've driven those things for thirty years. Thirty years! You get to know a machine after that long. You know the exact sound the hydraulics make when they need fluid. It's this high-pitched whine, almost like a mosquito right in your ear. My neighbor's kid, little Tommy, he got this electric scooter for Christmas, and it makes that exact same whining noise. Zips up and down the sidewalk all afternoon. I don't know how his parents stand it. Kids these days, they don't play outside like we used to, though, right? Unless it's got a motor or a screen. We used to play kick the can until the streetlights came on. You had to be fast, too, because old Mrs. Higgins down the block would call the cops if you stepped on her prize-winning petunias. She was serious about those flowers. Won a ribbon at the county fair three years running. The fair used to be such a big deal. They had this funnel cake stand that made them fresh, piping hot, covered in powdered sugar. You'd get sugar all down your shirt, but it was worth it. Now the fair is just overpriced rides that look like they're going to fall apart... but yeah, I'm on leave now. And even at home, I just can't keep up. My wife has had to take over paying all the bills, keeping track of my appointments, even most of the chores around the house. I just... I can't seem to manage it reliably anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face looks less expressive or that your voice sounds flatter than it used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my wife... she actually brought that up just the other day. She says my face has gone flat, like a board. Says I hardly answer her when she talks to me. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, just staring at my coffee instead of starting anything that needs doing. It's funny, I used to drink my coffee black, just straight black. But then a few years ago, my stomach started acting up, giving me this awful heartburn. The doctor told me to cut back on the acid, so I started putting a little splash of whole milk in it. Not cream, cream is too heavy. Just milk. We used to get milk delivered to the house when I was a boy. The milkman, Mr. Peterson, he drove this white truck with a picture of a cow on the side. He'd leave the glass bottles right on the porch in a little metal wire carrier. You had to bring the empty bottles back out the next morning so he could take them back to the dairy to be washed and reused. It made a lot of sense, really. Good for the environment, not like all these plastic jugs we use now that just end up floating in the ocean somewhere. I saw a documentary on TV about the ocean, about how there's this giant patch of garbage just swirling around out there in the Pacific. It's the size of Texas, they said. Can you imagine that? Just miles and miles of plastic bags and soda rings and old fishing nets. It makes you wonder what kind of world we're leaving for the next generation. My grandson, he's only four, and he loves those little juice boxes, you know the ones with the tiny straws wrapped in plastic? We buy them by the case. It's convenient, sure, but... what was I saying? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, she says I just look blank now. And I just sit there with my coffee.\n\nDoctor: When the voices say you’re dangerous, do you believe that’s true about yourself?\n\nPatient: Dangerous? I... I don't know if I'd say dangerous, but I know I'm a burden. I just sit there and I keep going over every single mistake I ever made at that warehouse, and all the ones at home, too. Like this one time, I clipped a pallet of canned peaches with the forks. Dented about forty cans. They had to sell them at a discount down at the surplus store. My wife loves that surplus store. She actually used to make this incredible peach cobbler on Sundays. Real thick crust, you know? You have to use ice-cold butter for a good pie crust. If the butter gets too warm, the pastry gets tough. My mother taught her that trick. My mother was a baker, actually. Worked at this little bakery on 4th Street that made the absolute best sourdough bread. People would line up around the block on Saturday mornings just to get a warm loaf. There was this one guy, wore a yellow raincoat no matter the weather, always bought three loaves and a dozen oatmeal cookies. Oatmeal raisin, not chocolate chip. I never understood that. Chocolate chip is clearly superior, especially if they're soft-baked. The crunchy ones just crumble all over your shirt and make a mess on the couch... but, anyway, I just feel like my family would have been so much better off if I hadn't let everyone down. I just ruined everything for them.\n\nDoctor: When the voices are happening, do they make you feel guilty, like you’ve done something wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do. I mean, when I was at my absolute lowest... I was barely eating a thing, I stopped going to choir practice entirely, and I just felt like I had failed everybody. And those messages from the scanners and the dispatch voices, they were happening right along with all of that, just piling on. \n\nI did miss choir, though. I sing baritone. We were practicing for the spring service, and our director, Martha, she had just ordered us these brand new robes. Burgundy with gold sashes. They looked real sharp, but I'll tell you, they were made of this heavy polyester. You start sweating the second you zip it up, especially since the church's air conditioning has been on the fritz since last summer. The building committee keeps dragging their feet on hiring a repair guy. They have to have a meeting just to schedule another meeting, you know? My buddy Phil is on the committee, and he says half the time they just argue about who's bringing the donuts. I always tell him to get apple fritters. None of that custard-filled nonsense. Custard goes bad so fast if you leave it sitting out in a warm room. You have to be real careful with dairy, it'll give you awful food poisoning if you aren't paying attention... \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh. Yeah, the voices and the scanner messages were definitely happening right at the same time I was feeling so down on myself.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not feeling depressed but were still hearing the dispatch voices?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... there was a stretch, I guess. My mood had eased up some, you know? I was actually eating decent meals again, talking a little more to my wife instead of just staring at the table. We even had chicken parmesan one night. She makes a really good chicken parmesan, pounds the cutlets real thin so they get crispy on the edges. You have to use the seasoned breadcrumbs, though, not the plain ones, or it just tastes like cardboard. And lots of mozzarella. We usually get the block of cheese and shred it ourselves. Those pre-shredded bags have some kind of powder on them to keep the cheese from clumping together, and it just doesn't melt right. It gets all greasy and separates. I saw a video online about how they make that anti-caking powder out of wood pulp. Cellulose, they call it. Can you believe they put wood in our food? It's just like those fast-food places using pink slime in the burgers. You really have to read the labels on everything these days, or you don't know what you're putting in your body. My neighbor, Gary, he started growing all his own vegetables in the backyard because he doesn't trust the grocery stores anymore. He's got these huge tomato plants, must be six feet tall, tied up to wooden stakes. He uses this special organic fertilizer that smells absolutely awful when it rains, just like... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the voices. Right. Yeah, even when I was feeling a bit better and eating the chicken parmesan, for several weeks I still heard dispatch calling my number. I still believed that routing system was tracking me, even when the depression wasn't so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that you have Parkinson’s disease, dementia, epilepsy, or another brain or nerve illness?\n\nPatient: No, no doctor has ever said anything like that to me. No Parkinson's or dementia or anything. I mean, my memory isn't what it used to be, but whose is at sixty-two? I misplace my keys sometimes, but that's just because my wife bought this new bowl for the entryway table. It's this ugly ceramic thing, painted bright orange with little green frogs on it. She got it at a craft fair down at the community center. They have those fairs every spring, right around the time the cherry blossoms bloom. The cherry trees down by the river are really something else. People come from all over just to take pictures with them. They have this whole festival with food trucks and live music. Last year, they had a bluegrass band that was actually pretty good. The banjo player was missing half his index finger, but you wouldn't know it from how fast he was picking. He said he lost it in a table saw accident when he was building a deck for his brother-in-law. You've got to be so careful with power tools. Always use the safety guard. My uncle didn't use the guard on his circular saw and it kicked back on him, took a chunk right out of his thigh. He had to get thirty stitches. The scar looked like a giant centipede... \n\nBut, uh, anyway, no, my brain is fine, far as any doctor has ever told me. No nerve illnesses or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, have you been hearing these dispatch voices nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's been every day for... gosh, way more than a month now. Even when I'm just sitting out in my garage, you know? I'll have the door down, everything shut off, dead quiet, and I still hear those dispatch voices clear as day. They'll call out my operator number and say I'm dangerous, or that security is on their way to come get me. It's real loud, too. \n\nI spend a lot of time in that garage. I've got a whole setup in there. Workbench, pegboard for all my tools. I spent a whole weekend last fall just organizing my sockets. You know how it is, you always lose the 10-millimeter one. I swear there's a black hole somewhere that just swallows up 10-millimeter sockets and guitar picks. I don't even play guitar, but I found three picks under the passenger seat of my truck when I was vacuuming it out last week. I take it down to that car wash on Elm Street, the one with the free vacuums. They say they're free, but the suction is terrible. You're better off just using a Shop-Vac at home. I bought a really nice Shop-Vac at a yard sale a few years back. Five bucks. The guy selling it was moving to Florida. Everybody moves to Florida eventually, it seems like. My sister went down to Tampa two years ago. She says the humidity is awful, makes her hair frizz up like a poodle. She sent me a picture of this alligator that was just sunning itself on her front lawn. Can you imagine? You walk out to get the morning paper and there's a dinosaur sitting in your grass. You'd have to call animal control, but I bet down there they get so many calls they don't even hurry. They probably just tell you to wait for it to leave. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, the voices. Yeah, every single day for months, telling me security is coming for me, even when I'm just sitting in the quiet garage.\n\nDoctor: Outside of feeling depressed, have there been times when you became very withdrawn or had little facial expression?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, my wife mentions that a lot. She says my face has gone completely flat, like I'm wearing a mask or something. And I hardly answer her when she talks to me. Most days I just sit at the kitchen table, staring into my coffee mug instead of starting any of the projects that need doing around the house. \n\nI drink a lot of coffee, though. Usually that French roast, the dark one. We used to get the breakfast blend, but it just tastes like hot water to me now. You really need a good, strong cup to wake up, you know? I use a French press. It makes a huge difference compared to those drip machines. The oils from the beans stay in the coffee instead of getting trapped in a paper filter. You have to make sure the water isn't boiling, though, or it burns the grounds and gets all bitter. I learned that from this barista at a little cafe downtown. We went there for our anniversary a couple of years ago. They had these incredible almond croissants. Flaky, with powdered sugar all over the top. I ended up getting powdered sugar all down the front of my good navy blazer. It's a real pain to get out, too. You can't just brush it off, it smears. I had to take it to the dry cleaner on 4th Street. The guy who runs it, Mr. Kim, he's been there forever. He always has these butterscotch candies in a little glass dish on the counter. The ones with the yellow wrappers that look like gold cellophane. My grandfather used to carry those in his pocket. They'd always get covered in lint, but we ate them anyway...\n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right. The withdrawn thing. Yeah, I just sit there staring at the coffee, not doing much of anything, and my wife says I don't show any expression anymore.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the dispatch voices even though you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, there was a time... I guess it was a few months back. My mood had actually eased up a bit, you know? I wasn't feeling so down in the dumps, and I was eating a little more, talking a bit more with my wife. We even went out to dinner once, to this little Italian place over on Main Street. Luigi's, I think it's called. They have this garlic bread that is just out of this world. They bake it with real butter, not that cheap margarine stuff, and they load it up with parmesan cheese. You can smell it before they even bring it to the table. The waiter we had, he was a young kid, maybe early twenties, going to the community college. He said he was studying criminal justice, wanted to be a state trooper. I told him he'd better get used to drinking bad coffee on the night shift. My buddy Dave was a cop for thirty years, and he always complained about the coffee at the station. He said it tasted like battery acid. Dave actually retired down to Arizona a few years ago. He bought this RV, one of those huge Class A motorhomes, and he just drives around the desert. He sends me pictures sometimes. The sunsets out there are incredible, all purple and orange. But I don't think I could handle the heat. I mean, dry heat or not, 110 degrees is just too hot. You can't even go outside without feeling like you're in an oven. I prefer having four seasons, even if the winters get a little rough. Last winter we had that ice storm that knocked out the power for three days. We had to huddle around the fireplace...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the voices. Yeah, so even during that stretch when my mood was better and I was eating and talking more, for several weeks I was still hearing the dispatch calling my number. Clear as a bell. And I still knew the routing system was tracking me, even when I wasn't feeling depressed."
